# Literature and novelty check: quadratic-Pisot Parry-rank folds

Checked: 2026-08-02 (Asia/Singapore).

## arXiv API query log

The required first-party Atom API was queried directly at
`https://export.arxiv.org/api/query` with the following searches:

- `all:(beta-expansion OR beta-numeration OR Pisot substitution)`, `max_results=50`;
- `au:Frougny`, `max_results=5`;
- `all:beta-expansion`, `max_results=10`.

Every request reached arXiv but returned HTTP `429` with
`cache-control: private, no-store` (Varnish edge: Singapore, upstream LGA).
Three delayed retries of the broad query returned the same status.  This is
recorded as an API availability limitation, not treated as a zero-result
novelty search.  To avoid a false novelty assertion, the audit was completed
against arXiv's first-party HTML search and abstract metadata, with DOI
metadata cross-checked through Crossref.

First-party arXiv exact-topic searches returned zero records for each of:

- `"finite-window beta fold"`;
- `"sliding-window beta normalization conjugacy"`;
- `"quadratic Pisot window threshold"`;
- `"Parry fold conjugacy"`;
- `"beta expansion sliding block conjugacy"`.

This supports only the narrow statement that no arXiv record located by these
queries states the paper's cyclic Parry-rank sliding-fold threshold.  It is
not proof that no unpublished or differently worded result exists.

## Closest beta-expansion and normalization literature

1. W. Parry, *On the beta-expansions of real numbers*, Acta Math. Acad. Sci.
   Hungar. **11** (1960), 401-416. DOI:
   `10.1007/BF02020954`. No arXiv record (publication predates arXiv).
   Used for the lexicographic admissibility criterion and `d_beta^*(1)`;
   the paper does not study cyclic finite-window folds or overlap thresholds.

2. C. Frougny, *Representations of numbers and finite automata*, Math.
   Systems Theory **25** (1992), 37-60. DOI:
   `10.1007/BF01305290`. No arXiv record located. Used only for the established
   finite-automaton normalization context; its automata are not presented as
   the cyclic rank-modulo-`Q_m` maps classified here.

3. C. Frougny and W. Steiner, *Minimal weight expansions in Pisot bases*,
   J. Math. Cryptology **2** (2008), 365-392. arXiv:`0803.2874`;
   DOI:`10.1515/JMC.2008.017`. It proves automaton recognizability for
   minimal-weight expansions in Pisot bases, not the present greedy
   Parry-rank fold or a sharp window threshold.

4. V. Berthe, C. Frougny, M. Rigo and J. Sakarovitch, *The carry propagation
   of the successor function*, Adv. Appl. Math. **120** (2020), 102062.
   arXiv:`1907.01464`; DOI:`10.1016/j.aam.2020.102062`. It studies amortized
   carry propagation in several numeration systems, including beta-numeration;
   it does not classify sliding-window conjugacy.

5. T. Hejda and W. Steiner, *Beta-expansions of rational numbers in quadratic
   Pisot bases*, Acta Arith. **183** (2018), 35-51.
   arXiv:`1411.2419`; DOI:`10.4064/aa8260-11-2017`. This is the closest
   full-quadratic-Pisot parameter comparison found. It studies purely periodic
   rational beta-expansions and computes `gamma(beta)`, not finite-window
   normalization or overlap injectivity.

6. B. Adamczewski, C. Frougny, A. Siegel and W. Steiner, *Rational numbers
   with purely periodic beta-expansion*, Bull. Lond. Math. Soc. **42** (2010),
   538-552. arXiv:`0907.0206`; DOI:`10.1112/blms/bdq019`. Relevant to
   periodic beta-expansions and Pisot-unit restrictions, but not to the cyclic
   fold threshold.

7. C. Kalle and W. Steiner, *Beta-expansions, natural extensions and multiple
   tilings associated with Pisot units*, Trans. Amer. Math. Soc. **364**
   (2012), 2281-2318. arXiv:`0907.2676`;
   DOI:`10.1090/S0002-9947-2012-05362-1`. It develops natural extensions and
   tilings arising from greedy beta-transformations; the abstract explicitly
   traces that geometric construction to Rauzy and Thurston. It does not
   contain the rank-modulo finite-window code studied here.

8. M. Minervino and W. Steiner, *Tilings for Pisot beta numeration*,
   arXiv:`1310.1277`. This treats Rauzy/beta tiles, weak finiteness, and purely
   periodic expansions for non-unit Pisot numbers; no DOI was exposed in the
   arXiv metadata checked. It supplies context for non-unit bases, not the
   claimed threshold.

## Thurston and Pisot-substitution boundary

W. Thurston's *Groups, tilings, and finite state automata* (AMS Colloquium
Lectures, 1989) is an unpublished lecture manuscript commonly cited for
beta-tiles; no arXiv ID or DOI was located.  The present manuscript therefore
does not assign it a fabricated identifier.  The traceable modern source used
for the Rauzy-Thurston statement is Kalle-Steiner, arXiv:`0907.2676`, DOI
`10.1090/S0002-9947-2012-05362-1`.

For the broader substitution interface, representative records are:

- M. Minervino and J. Thuswaldner, *The geometry of non-unit Pisot
  substitutions*, arXiv:`1402.2002`, Ann. Inst. Fourier **64** (2014),
  1373-1417, DOI:`10.5802/aif.2884`;
- V. Berthe, W. Steiner and J. Thuswaldner, *Geometry, dynamics, and
  arithmetic of S-adic shifts*, arXiv:`1410.0331`, Ann. Inst. Fourier **69**
  (2019), 1347-1409, DOI:`10.5802/aif.3273`;
- K. Nakaishi, *Pisot Substitution Conjecture and Rauzy Fractals*,
  arXiv:`2401.07771` (no DOI in the checked arXiv metadata).

These concern Rauzy fractals, substitutions, tilings, or spectral questions.
They are not predecessors of the cyclic finite-window rank fold.  Accordingly
the theorem is explicitly restricted to greedy quadratic beta-shifts and does
not claim a threshold for general Pisot substitutions.

## Novelty conclusion and limitation

The exact result not found in the checked records is the classification of the
least nontrivial aperture at which the cyclic Parry-rank fold from the full
digit shift is a conjugacy onto its SFT image, together with the two extremal
quadratic-Pisot loci.  Parry admissibility, Pisot normalization automata,
periodic expansions, beta-tiles, and Pisot-substitution geometry are cited as
prior results and are not reproved as new.  Because the Atom API itself was
rate-limited, the novelty conclusion remains a documented search conclusion,
not a claim of exhaustive bibliographic nonexistence.

## Follow-up API audit for the local-structure results

Checked: 2026-08-03 (Asia/Singapore).  The first-party Atom endpoint was
available for this audit.  Each request returned HTTP 200.  The exact query
`all:"cyclic rank recoding"` returned zero records.  The following broader
queries also returned zero records:

- `all:("quadratic Pisot" AND "sliding block")`;
- `all:("quadratic Pisot" AND "causal decoding")`;
- `all:("beta-expansion" AND "finite window" AND conjugacy)`;
- `all:("bounded coefficient" AND "polynomial multiple")`;
- `all:("Fischer cover" AND "full shift")`;
- `all:("Parry" AND "quadratic Pisot" AND language)`;
- `all:("beta-shifts" AND "same language")`.

The query `all:("near Markov" AND sofic)` returned one record,
Marcus--Meyerovitch--Wu, *A Krieger Embedding Theorem for Near Markov Sofic
Shifts*, arXiv:2606.25475.  It proves an embedding theorem for the established
near-Markov class; it does not contain the cyclic Parry-rank recoding, the
two-fixed-point extremal family, or its Fischer presentation.  The query
`au:Jankauskas_J AND all:Littlewood` returned Drungilas--Jankauskas--
Junevicius--Klebonas--Siurys, *On certain multiples of Littlewood and Newman
polynomials*, arXiv:1801.07179.  That paper studies divisibility between
Littlewood and Newman polynomials.  It does not identify a bounded multiple
degree with the causal inverse length of a sliding cyclic rank code.

These searches locate no arXiv predecessor for: the aperture-two
cross-chamber equality and aperture-three separation law; the exact causal
lengths two and three; the family-specific critical two-fixed-point normal
form; or the exact finite-block onset and Markov order.  The manuscript cites
Parry admissibility, bounded-polynomial-multiple terminology, near-Markov
terminology, and the general Fischer-cover criterion as prior framework and
claims only the parameter-specific deductions for its newly defined recoding.

## Independent refresh for the cubic upgrade and named nearest work

Checked: 2026-08-08 (Asia/Singapore).

The arXiv Atom API was queried for `"cyclic language rank"`, `"cubic
Pisot" AND "causal"`, `"Bertrand numeration" AND "sliding block"`,
`"finite delay" AND "beta-expansion"`, and the exact Bassino title.  The
endpoint returned HTTP 429 with `cache-control: private, no-store` through the
LGA/Singapore Varnish route.  This is an API availability failure, not a
zero-result search.  Google Scholar title/mechanism searches and Crossref
bibliographic searches were therefore used as independent discovery checks.

Crossref confirmed the following nearest-work records and DOI metadata:

- P. B. A. Lecomte and M. Rigo, *Numeration Systems on a Regular Language*,
  Theory Comput. Systems **34** (2001), 27--44,
  DOI `10.1007/s002240010014`.  Its published abstract introduces abstract
  numeration by ordering an arbitrary infinite regular language and studies
  finite-automaton recognizability.  It supplies the ordered-language stage,
  not cyclic reduction modulo a fixed-length language count or overlap
  injectivity.
- V. Bruyere and G. Hansel, *Bertrand numeration systems and
  recognizability*, Theoret. Comput. Sci. **181** (1997), 17--43,
  DOI `10.1016/S0304-3975(96)00260-5`.  Its 26-item Crossref reference trail
  includes Bertrand-Mathis, Parry, Frougny, Cobham, Shallit, and the classical
  automata/linear-numeration literature.  It concerns Bertrand systems and
  recognizable sets, not the manuscript's rank-modulo-`Q_m` sliding map.
- F. Bassino, *Beta-Expansions for Cubic Pisot Numbers*, LATIN 2002,
  pp. 141--152, DOI `10.1007/3-540-45995-2_17`.  The abstract states that the
  expansion of one is computed for every cubic Pisot number and that cubic
  simple beta-numbers are Pisot.  Its references include Akiyama, Boyd,
  Frougny--Solomyak, Hollander, Parry, Schmidt, and Solomyak.  Consequently,
  the Pisot/simple-Parry expansion data for the candidate cubic family are
  published input, not a novelty claim.

Exact Google Scholar searches for `"cyclic language rank" Pisot` and for the
polynomial string `"x^3-(n+2)x^2+2nx-n"` returned no matching scholarly
title.  The broader search `"cubic Pisot" "sliding block"` returned work on
beta-shifts/S-gap shifts and general automata material, not a cyclic
language-rank fold.  Crossref searches for `cubic Pisot causal inverse`,
`finite delay sliding block beta expansion`, and the exact polynomial found
Bassino and established beta-expansion/arithmetic papers but no causal-depth
or overlap-threshold predecessor.

The defensible novelty boundary is therefore narrow.  Ordered regular-
language numeration, Bertrand recognizability, cubic Pisot expansions, and
simple-Parry admissibility are prior art.  The new cubic statement is the
parameter-uniform modulus-`Q_m` collision-depth theorem.  Its required
all-parameter two-window carry exclusion and terminal-path induction are now
proved in the manuscript; no novelty is claimed for Bassino's expansion data.

The zbMATH Open API supplied the MathSciNet-style subject-index check.  Exact
title searches returned Lecomte--Rigo as Zbl `0969.68095`,
Bruyere--Hansel as Zbl `0957.11015`, and Bassino as Zbl `1152.11342`.
The Bassino query also returned Akiyama's *Cubic Pisot units with finite beta
expansions* (Zbl `1001.11038`) and the established periodic-expansion
literature.  Searches for `cubic Pisot causal inverse` and `cyclic language
rank Pisot` returned no zbMATH record.  These negative results have the same
query-language limitation as the other database searches and are not treated
as proof of bibliographic nonexistence.

Nearest prior work remains Fr\'ed\'erique Bassino, *Beta-Expansions for Cubic
Pisot Numbers*, LATIN 2002.  It supplies the cubic Pisot/simple-Parry

## Tier-2 audit: externally posed open problems and actual interfaces

Checked: 2026-08-14 (Asia/Singapore).

### Search record and open status

The arXiv Atom API was queried for Salem-number beta expansions, purely
periodic Pisot expansions, weak finiteness, Property (F), and the Pisot
substitution conjecture.  Primary sources arXiv:1411.2419, arXiv:1912.10386,
arXiv:1810.03500, arXiv:2206.06675, and arXiv:2603.19554 were inspected in
full text.  Crossref and OpenAlex returned HTTP 429 and are recorded as
rate-limited, not as zero-result searches.  Semantic Scholar returned the
records for DOI `10.4064/aa8260-11-2017`, `10.1112/blms/12.4.269`, and
`10.1017/etds.2016.44`; its citation graph lists only two citations of
Hejda--Steiner, neither claiming a solution of the questions below.  The
zbMATH Open API was searched for `purely periodic Pisot`, `Pisot substitution
conjecture`, and `Salem Parry Schmidt`.  Its post-2018 real-base results do
not report a resolution of the Hejda--Steiner questions.  Its review of E.
Miyanohara, *A note on non-periodic greedy expansions in Salem base*, Res.
Number Theory **10** (2024), DOI `10.1007/s40993-024-00507-8`, still calls
Salem eventual periodicity an open question.  The 2022 result arXiv:2206.06675
proves that a positive-density set of powers of a Salem number are Parry,
not that the Salem number itself is Parry.  These checks support the open
status assertions below as of the audit date.

### Four printed questions

1. **Schmidt's Salem--Parry conjecture.**  K. Schmidt, *On periodic
   expansions of Pisot numbers and Salem numbers*, Bull. Lond. Math. Soc.
   **12** (1980), 269--278, DOI `10.1112/blms/12.4.269`.  Stockton's explicit
   statement of Schmidt's conjecture in *On the co-factors of degree 6 Salem
   number beta expansions*, Int. J. Number Theory **17** (2021), 1175--1200,
   DOI `10.1142/S1793042121500317`, arXiv:1912.10386, reads: "Schmidt
   conjectured that the same was true for
   Salem numbers," where "the same" is that `beta` is a Parry number, i.e.
   `d_beta(1)` is eventually periodic.  Stockton adds: "it is still not known
   for any degree greater than 4."

2. **Hejda--Steiner Question (A).**  T. Hejda and W. Steiner,
   *Beta-expansions of rational numbers in quadratic Pisot bases*, Acta Arith.
   **183** (2018), 35--51, DOI `10.4064/aa8260-11-2017`, arXiv:1411.2419,
   Section 5, final open-problems list: "Prove or disprove that
   $\gamma(\beta)=1$ for quadratic Pisot number
   $\beta>1$, a root of $\beta^2=a\beta+b$, if and only if
   $a/b\in\mathbb Z$ and $a\ge b^2$ or
   $(a,b)\in\{(24,6),(30,6)\}$."

3. **Hejda--Steiner Question (B).**  Same citation and open-problems list:
   "For which
   quadratic $\beta$ we have that $\gamma(\beta)=0$? Can we drop the
   restrictions on $a$ and $b$ in Theorem 2? More specifically, is it true
   that $a<\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2}b$ implies $\gamma(\beta)=0$?"

4. **Hejda--Steiner Question (C).**  Same citation and open-problems list:
   "What is the
   structure of the prefixes of $\beta$-adic expansions of integers for a
   general quadratic $\beta$?"

### Machinery-to-problem map

- **Schmidt.**  The finite Parry word and recurrence used in
  `thm:simple-parry-causal-collision` exist only after the desired eventual
  periodicity (and, in the paper, the stronger simple-Parry property) is
  known.  The collision graph can analyze a Salem base already known to be
  simple Parry, but it supplies no bounded orbit for `T_beta(1)`.  The missing
  step is exactly the conjecture, so applying the graph would be circular.
- **Question (A).**  `prop:parry-rank-bijection` and
  `thm:full-quadratic-pisot-threshold` cover precisely the roots
  `beta^2=a beta+b` and give their legal languages and count recurrence.
  However, `gamma(beta)` is defined by equality of real beta-values and the
  beta-natural-extension domain.  The paper reduces an integer language rank
  modulo `Q_m`.  A bridge would have to identify these congruence fibers with
  beta-adic/natural-extension fibers or establish the required tile-boundary
  inclusion; no such identity is present, and the paper gives an explicit
  example showing rank congruence does not preserve beta-value.
- **Question (B).**  The nearest-multiple separation lemma controls multiples
  of the integer word count `Q_m`, not the conjugate-coordinate extrema that
  determine `gamma(beta)`.  What is missing is a uniform beta-tile boundary
  estimate in the parameter range `a<phi b`.  None of the sliding congruence
  estimates has that geometric conclusion.
- **Question (C).**  The closest objects are the standard Parry language,
  its colex rank, and the prefix counts `Q_m`.  The paper's cyclic rank fold
  discards the compatible inverse-system data needed for a beta-adic integer.
  A canonical map would require compatibility of the residues as `m` varies;
  the current moduli `Q_m` do not divide one another, and no bonding maps are
  proved.  Thus the finite-window results do not determine beta-adic prefix
  structure.

### A/B/C/D assessment and selected route

- **(A):** no named problem above is honestly touched beyond sharing the
  Parry language.  The numerical-value, natural-extension, or substitution
  geometry needed by each is absent.
- **(B):** the input full shift, greedy beta-language, Parry word, language
  counts, and colex ordered-language rank are standard.  The reduction modulo
  `Q_m` is bespoke.  Above threshold the image is canonically conjugate back
  to a full shift, so it does not yield a new invariant of a standard
  beta-system.  There is no canonical map to numerical beta-normalization.
- **(C):** the Pisot hypothesis in the exact collision criterion, causal
  completeness, bounded-multiple order, and aperture-two trichotomy is an
  artifact: their proofs use only simple-Parry language/rank data and finite
  congruence graphs.  The simple-Parry hypothesis itself is real because it
  supplies the finite recurrence and positional colex rank used by the graph.
- **(D):** the negative-conjugate conjecture `ell_cau(beta,m)=2` for `m>=4`
  remains plausible, but a proof would sharpen only the bespoke decoder and
  the current carry analysis does not exhaust the nonzero constant-carry
  branches.  No plausible converse links overlap injectivity to a standard
  beta-expansion property.

Route **(C)** was selected as the best product of feasibility and impact.
Proposition `prop:nonpisot-simple-parry-scope` removes Pisot from the four
general simple-Parry results.  Strictness is proved using the irreducible
non-Pisot simple-Parry polynomial `x^4-2x^3-2`, for which
`d_beta(1)=2002 0^infinity` and `ell_cau(beta,2)=2`.  This is a genuine
hypothesis removal, but it does not solve a named problem and does not by
itself move the paper to Tier 2.

# Reproducing the finite checks

Run all commands from the paper directory. The stored run used Python 3.10.11.
The verifiers use only the Python standard library, and the computations use
deterministic exact integer arithmetic apart from displayed algebraic-root
approximations.

## Verification commands

```sh
python -m unittest discover -s artifacts -p "test_*.py" -v
python artifacts/verify_metallic_threshold.py --output artifacts/metallic_threshold_verification.txt
python artifacts/verify_quadratic_pisot_threshold.py --output artifacts/quadratic_pisot_threshold_verification.txt
python artifacts/verify_simple_parry_causal.py --output artifacts/simple_parry_causal_verification.txt
```

The unit suite prints `Ran 30 tests` followed by `OK`. The verifier conclusions
are:

```text
SUMMARY: 0 failures / 0 counterexamples
SUMMARY: 0 failures / 0 unexpected collisions
SUMMARY: 0 failures
```

All three verifiers exit zero and write Unix LF reports. The reports contain no
clock or date fields.

## Checksum verification

Paths in `SHA256SUMS` are relative to the `artifacts` directory. From the paper
directory, run exactly:

```sh
cd artifacts && sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS
```

\section{Cyclic rank recoding for quadratic Pisot languages}
\label{sec:quadratic-pisot-extension}

This section closes the interface left by Remark~\ref{rem:metallic-beta-scope}.
The point requiring care is that a quadratic Pisot number need not be a unit
and its conjugate need not be negative.  Consequently the boundary-modified
constant-type language is not the appropriate common object.  We instead use
the greedy $\beta$-language and its canonical Parry--Bertrand positional rank.
This rank belongs to the established theory of nonintegral-base numeration
\cite{BertrandMathis1989,BruyereHansel1997,LecomteRigo2001}; the
canonical/noncanonical distinction for simple-Parry systems is clarified in
\cite{CharlierCisterninoStipulanti2022}.  We give a direct interval proof for
the two quadratic chambers because the exact endpoints are used later.  The
operation introduced here begins only when the fixed-length rank is reduced
cyclically modulo $Q_m$ and consecutive windows are overlapped.  This
recoding is not numerical
$\beta$-value normalization: it asserts congruence only between integer
language ranks and does not assert equality in $\mathbb Z[\beta]$.  The
classical $\beta$-shift and automata literature supplies context
\cite{Renyi1957,ItoTakahashi1974,Parry1960,Blanchard1989,Loraud1995};
the map-specific sliding-congruence statements used below are proved here.

\subsection{Quadratic classification and canonical finite windows}

\begin{lemma}[The two quadratic Pisot parameter chambers]
\label{lem:quadratic-pisot-chambers}
Let $\beta>1$ be a quadratic algebraic integer and let $\beta'$ be its
conjugate.  Then $|\beta'|<1$ if and only if the minimal polynomial of
$\beta$ belongs to exactly one of the following two families:
\begin{align*}
P^-_{a,b}(x)&=x^2-ax-b, & a\ge b\ge1,\\
P^+_{a,b}(x)&=x^2-ax+b, & a\ge3,\quad 1\le b\le a-2.
\end{align*}
In the first chamber $\beta'<0$, $\lfloor\beta\rfloor=a$, and
\[
d_\beta(1)=ab0^\infty,\qquad
d_\beta^*(1)=\bigl(a(b-1)\bigr)^\infty.
\]
In the second chamber $\beta'>0$, $\lfloor\beta\rfloor=a-1$, and
\[
d_\beta(1)=(a-1)(a-b-1)^\infty=d_\beta^*(1).
\]
Here $d_\beta$ is the greedy expansion and $d_\beta^*$ is Parry's
quasi-greedy boundary word.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write the monic minimal polynomial as $x^2-ax+c$.  Since
$c=\beta\beta'\ne0$, the sign of $c$ is the sign of $\beta'$.  If
$c=-b<0$, then $b/\beta=|\beta'|<1$.  The positive root exceeds $a$;
the last inequality is equivalent, for integral $a,b$, to $1\le b\le a$.
Conversely these inequalities place the negative root in $(-1,0)$.
If $c=b>0$, then $0<b/\beta=\beta'<1$ and $a-1<\beta<a$; evaluating
$P^+_{a,b}$ at $a-1$ and $a$ gives exactly $a\ge3$ and
$1\le b\le a-2$.  The converses follow from the same sign test.

For $P^-_{a,b}$ one has $\beta^2=a\beta+b$.  The greedy algorithm applied
to $1$ therefore gives the two digits $a,b$ and then terminates; Parry's
standard replacement of a finite expansion gives
$d_\beta^*(1)=(a(b-1))^\infty$.  For $P^+_{a,b}$, put
$r=\beta-a+1=1-b/\beta$.  Then
$\lfloor\beta\rfloor=a-1$ and
\[
\beta r=\beta-b=(a-b-1)+r,
\]
so every digit after the first is $a-b-1$.  The expansion is infinite and
hence already quasi-greedy.  This is precisely Parry's calculation
\cite{Parry1960}; it is included to fix the parameters, not as a new proof of
his general admissibility theorem.
\end{proof}

For either chamber put $d=\lfloor\beta\rfloor$ and
$\mathcal A_\beta=\{0,\ldots,d\}$.  Let $\mathcal L_m(X_\beta)$ be the
length-$m$ language of the two-sided greedy $\beta$-shift, characterized by
Parry's suffix inequalities against $d_\beta^*(1)$.

For words $x=(x_1,\ldots,x_m)$ and $y=(y_1,\ldots,y_m)$ in low-to-high
orientation, write $x<_{\rm colex}y$ when
$(x_m,\ldots,x_1)<_{\rm lex}(y_m,\ldots,y_1)$.  Lexicographic comparison of a
finite word with an infinite boundary uses the available finite prefix: the
first unequal digit decides the comparison, and equality through the end of
the finite word is allowed.  A comparison with $c_0^m$ is the literal
finite-word comparison.

\begin{definition}[Cyclic rank recoding attached to the Parry language]
\label{def:quadratic-parry-fold}
For $m\ge0$ define $Q_m(\beta)=|\mathcal L_m(X_\beta)|$; the recurrences for
these counts are proved in Proposition~\ref{prop:parry-rank-bijection}.
Use the low-to-high orientation
\[
X_m^{(\beta)}:=\{x\in\mathcal A_\beta^m:
(x_m,\ldots,x_1)\in\mathcal L_m(X_\beta)\}
\]
and set
\[
\operatorname{Rank}_{\beta,m}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}x_{j+1}Q_j(\beta).
\]
The cyclic language-rank recoding is
\[
\Fold_{\beta,m}(w)=
\bigl(\operatorname{Rank}_{\beta,m}|_{X_m^{(\beta)}}\bigr)^{-1}
\left(\operatorname{Rank}_{\beta,m}(w)\bmod Q_m(\beta)\right).
\]
Finally,
\[
(\Phi_{\beta,m}(z))_t
=\Fold_{\beta,m}(z_t,\ldots,z_{t+m-1}),\qquad
Y_{\beta,m}=\Phi_{\beta,m}(\mathcal A_\beta^{\mathbb Z}).
\]
The nontrivial overlap threshold is
\[
m_*(\beta)=\min\{m\ge2:\Phi_{\beta,m}\text{ is a conjugacy onto the
SFT }Y_{\beta,m}\}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Canonical Parry--Bertrand rank in two quadratic chambers]
\label{prop:parry-rank-bijection}
The language counts satisfy
\begin{align}
Q_0&=1,\quad Q_1=a+1,\quad Q_m=aQ_{m-1}+bQ_{m-2}
&& (P^-_{a,b},\ m\ge2),\label{eq:qminus}\\
Q_0&=1,\quad Q_1=a,\quad Q_m=aQ_{m-1}-bQ_{m-2}
&& (P^+_{a,b},\ m\ge2).\label{eq:qplus}
\end{align}
For every quadratic Pisot $\beta$ and $m\ge1$,
\[
\operatorname{Rank}_{\beta,m}|_{X_m^{(\beta)}}:
X_m^{(\beta)}\longrightarrow\{0,\ldots,Q_m(\beta)-1\}
\]
is an order-preserving bijection when $X_m^{(\beta)}$ is read in the
colexicographic order.  This is the canonical Parry--Bertrand positional
rank; the proof below is a direct two-chamber interval proof of that standard
structure.  Thus Definition~\ref{def:quadratic-parry-fold} is
determined by the greedy language and its Parry word; it is not a choice of
an auxiliary enumeration.  For $P^-_{a,1}$ it is exactly
Definition~\ref{def:metallic-window-data}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In the negative chamber, Parry admissibility reduces in the low-to-high
orientation to
\[
x_k=a\Longrightarrow x_{k-1}<b.
\]
We use induction on $m$, with the invariant that the ranks of $X_m^{(\beta)}$
form the initial interval $[0,Q_m-1]$.  It is immediate for $m=0,1$.  For
$m\ge2$, a high digit $h\in\{0,\ldots,a-1\}$ leaves an arbitrary word of
$X_{m-1}^{(\beta)}$ and hence contributes
\[
[hQ_{m-1},(h+1)Q_{m-1}-1].
\]
For high digit $a$, the next digit is
$g\in\{0,\ldots,b-1\}$ and the remaining word is arbitrary in
$X_{m-2}^{(\beta)}$; its ranks are
\[
aQ_{m-1}+[gQ_{m-2},(g+1)Q_{m-2}-1].
\]
The intervals are consecutive in colexicographic order and their union is
$[0,aQ_{m-1}+bQ_{m-2}-1]$.  This proves simultaneously the negative
recurrence, the induction invariant, and order preservation.

For the positive chamber put
\[
d=a-1,\qquad c_0=a-b-1,
\]
and define the boundary-bounded sublanguage
\[
B_m:=\{x\in X_m^{(\beta)}:
(x_m,\ldots,x_1)\le_{\rm lex}c_0^m\},
\qquad R_m:=|B_m|.
\]
We prove simultaneously, for every $m\ge0$, the coupled induction invariant
\begin{equation}
\Rank_{\beta,m}(X_m^{(\beta)})=[0,Q_m-1],
\qquad
\Rank_{\beta,m}(B_m)=[0,R_m-1].
\label{eq:coupled-rank-invariant}
\end{equation}
Here $X_0^{(\beta)}=B_0=\{\varnothing\}$, so $Q_0=R_0=1$ and the assertion
holds at $m=0$.  At $m=1$, one has $Q_1=d+1=a$ and
$R_1=c_0+1=a-b$, and the ranks are the stated intervals.

Assume \eqref{eq:coupled-rank-invariant} through length $m-1$.  Parry
admissibility says that a high digit $h<d$ may be followed by any word in
$X_{m-1}^{(\beta)}$, whereas a high digit $d$ must be followed by a word in
$B_{m-1}$.  The classes $h=0,\ldots,d-1$ therefore give the consecutive
intervals
\[
[hQ_{m-1},(h+1)Q_{m-1}-1],
\]
and the high class $h=d$ gives
\[
[dQ_{m-1},dQ_{m-1}+R_{m-1}-1].
\]
Consequently
\begin{equation}
Q_m=dQ_{m-1}+R_{m-1},
\qquad
\Rank_{\beta,m}(X_m^{(\beta)})=[0,Q_m-1].
\label{eq:positive-X-coupling}
\end{equation}

The same decomposition inside $B_m$ has high digits
$0,\ldots,c_0-1$ followed by arbitrary words of $X_{m-1}^{(\beta)}$, and
high digit $c_0$ followed by a word of $B_{m-1}$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
R_m=c_0Q_{m-1}+R_{m-1},
\qquad
\Rank_{\beta,m}(B_m)=[0,R_m-1].
\label{eq:positive-B-coupling}
\end{equation}
This closes the simultaneous induction and proves consecutiveness rather than
assuming it.  Subtracting \eqref{eq:positive-B-coupling} from
\eqref{eq:positive-X-coupling} gives
\[
Q_m-R_m=(d-c_0)Q_{m-1}=bQ_{m-1}.
\]
Thus, for $m\ge2$,
\[
R_{m-1}=Q_{m-1}-bQ_{m-2}
\]
and
\[
Q_m=dQ_{m-1}+R_{m-1}
=(d+1)Q_{m-1}-bQ_{m-2}
=aQ_{m-1}-bQ_{m-2},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:qplus}.  The order of the displayed digit classes is
the colexicographic order, so the same induction proves order preservation.
When $b=1$ in the negative chamber, the rule and recurrence are precisely
those of Definition~\ref{def:metallic-window-data}.
\end{proof}


\input{sections/05b_quadratic_pisot_separation}
\input{sections/05b_quadratic_pisot_local_structure}
$v\to\mathbf0$ exists, the overlap conditions give
$v_1=\cdots=v_{m-2}=0$, and the edge congruence reduces to
$v_0\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}$.  Since $|v_0|\le d<Q_m$, necessarily
$v=\mathbf0$.  Hence the zero vertex has the zero vertex as its unique
predecessor and is not reachable from $B$.

If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, the last assertion of
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} now gives a finite
future-only inverse.  The converse is immediate because a decoder recovers every raw
coordinate.  A directed cycle reachable from $B$ cannot contain
$\mathbf0$; repeating its first-coordinate labels gives a nonzero periodic
difference sequence and hence, by taking positive and negative parts, two
distinct periodic raw points with the same image.  Conversely, from any
nonzero bi-infinite collision path, shift to a vertex in $B$.  Its forward
path avoids $\mathbf0$ and therefore repeats among the
$(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ nonzero vertices, producing a reachable directed cycle.
This proves all three equivalences and the periodic certificate, whose cycle
may be chosen simple.

In the injective case the forward subgraph reachable from $B$ is acyclic and
contains at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ vertices.  A directed path in it has at
most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-2$ edges.  Formula
\eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} then gives
\eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Complete aperture-two trichotomy]
\label{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two}
Set
\[
c_\beta=t_2+\ind_{\{p\ge3\}}.
\]
Then $1\le c_\beta\le d+1$ and
\[
Q_2=d(d+1)+c_\beta.
\]
Exactly one of the following alternatives holds.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
  \item If $c_\beta=d+1$, then $\Fold_{\beta,2}$ is the identity on
  $\mathcal A_d^2$ and $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=1$.
  \item If $1\le c_\beta<d$, then $\Phi_{\beta,2}$ is injective and
  $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
  \item If $c_\beta=d$, then $\Phi_{\beta,2}$ is noninjective.  Its sole
  nonsingleton path-space fiber is
  \[
  \Phi_{\beta,2}(0^{\mathbb Z})
  =\Phi_{\beta,2}(d^{\mathbb Z})
  =((0,0))^{\mathbb Z},
  \]
  and $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
For $p=2$ the three cases are determined by $c_\beta=t_2$; for
$p\ge3$ they are determined by $c_\beta=t_2+1$.  In particular, a binary
simple-Parry word of order at least three has an identity aperture-two fold
when $t_2=1$ and exactly the displayed constant branch pair when $t_2=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $q=d+1$ and $M=Q_2=dq+c_\beta$.  The formula for $Q_2$ follows directly
from \eqref{eq:simple-parry-Q}, with the boundary summand $1$ present exactly
when $2<p$.  Since $t_2\ge1$ when $p=2$, whereas $0\le t_2\le d$ when
$p\ge3$, the stated range for $c_\beta$ follows.

An edge of $\Gamma_{\beta,2}$ is a pair $(e,f)\in\Delta_d^2$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
e+qf\equiv0\pmod M.
\label{eq:aperture-two-edge-congruence}
\end{equation}
The left side has modulus at most
$d(1+q)=q^2-1<2M$, so it equals $-M$, $0$, or $M$.  If it is zero, then
$q>|e|$ forces $(e,f)=(0,0)$.  If it is $M$, the bound $e\le d$ forces
$f=d$, because $f\le d-1$ would give
$M-qf\ge q+c_\beta>d$; hence $(e,f)=(c_\beta,d)$, which is admissible
exactly when $c_\beta\le d$.  The case $-M$ is its negative.  Thus the full
edge set is
\[
\{(0,0)\}\quad(c_\beta=d+1),
\qquad
\{(0,0),\ \pm(c_\beta,d)\}\quad(c_\beta\le d).
\]

When $c_\beta=d+1$, one has $Q_2=q^2=|\mathcal A_d^2|$, so every raw
length-two word is legal and cyclic reduction does nothing.  This proves
(a).  If $c_\beta<d$, the two nonzero edges cannot be followed by another
edge, because their terminal vertices are $\pm d$, not $\pm c_\beta$.
There is a one-output collision but no two-output collision, so
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} gives (b).  Finally, when
$c_\beta=d$, the two nonzero edges are the loops $(d,d)$ and $(-d,-d)$.
They give precisely the constant difference sequences $\pm d^{\mathbb Z}$.
The only raw realization of either is the pair
$\{0^{\mathbb Z},d^{\mathbb Z}\}$, and the common residue is zero in every
window.  No other nonzero bi-infinite path exists, proving (c) and the fiber
classification.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Pisot-free scope is strict]
\label{prop:nonpisot-simple-parry-scope}
Proposition~\ref{prop:simple-parry-bounded-order},
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}, Corollary
\ref{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}, and Theorem
\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} hold for every nonintegral simple-Parry
number; no Pisot hypothesis is required.  This strictly enlarges their scope.
Indeed, let $\beta$ be the positive root of
\begin{equation}
x^4-2x^3-2=0.
\label{eq:nonpisot-simple-parry-witness}
\end{equation}
Then
\[
d_\beta(1)=2002\,0^\infty,
\]
$\beta$ is not Pisot, and the aperture-two cyclic rank fold is injective with
$\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The recurrence for $Q_n$, the consecutive colex-rank formula, and Parry
admissibility used above hold for every simple-Parry number
\cite{Parry1960,CharlierCisterninoStipulanti2022}.  The proofs of the four
listed results subsequently use only these data, the full raw alphabet
$\{0,\ldots,d\}$, integer congruences modulo $Q_m$, and finite directed
graphs.  No estimate on a Galois conjugate occurs.  This proves the claimed
hypothesis removal.

It remains to show that the enlargement is strict.  The polynomial in
\eqref{eq:nonpisot-simple-parry-witness} is Eisenstein at $2$, hence
irreducible.  Its positive root satisfies
\[
2<\beta<\frac{11}{5},
\]
because the polynomial takes the values $-2$ and $81/625$ at the two
endpoints.  Its defining equation gives
\[
1=2\beta^{-1}+2\beta^{-4}.
\]
The proper suffixes of $2002\,0^\infty$ are all lexicographically smaller
than $2002\,0^\infty$: the first two begin with $0$, while the third is
$2000^\infty<2002\,0^\infty$.  Parry's criterion therefore gives
$d_\beta(1)=2002\,0^\infty$.

Write the negative real root as $-u$.  The function
$g(u)=u^4+2u^3-2$ is strictly increasing for $u>0$, with $g(0)<0<g(1)$,
so $0<u<1$.  To count all real roots, put
$f(x)=x^4-2x^3-2$.  Its derivative is
\[
f'(x)=2x^2(2x-3).
\]
Thus $f$ is strictly decreasing on $(-\infty,3/2)$ apart from the stationary
flattening at $0$, which does not change monotonicity, and is strictly
increasing on $(3/2,\infty)$.  Since $f(x)\to+\infty$ as
$x\to-\infty$, $f(0)=-2$, and $f(x)\to+\infty$ as $x\to+\infty$, there is
exactly one negative real root and exactly one positive real root.  The two
remaining roots are therefore a nonreal conjugate pair $z,\overline z$.
Moreover,
\[
\beta^4 g(2/\beta)
=4(3+4\beta-\beta^3)>0,
\]
where the last inequality follows from
$\beta<11/5$ and
$3+4\beta-\beta^3>11-(11/5)^3=44/125$.
Thus $u<2/\beta$.  The product of the four roots is $-2$, whence
\[
|z|^2=\frac{2}{\beta u}>1.
\]
Irreducibility makes $z$ a Galois conjugate of $\beta$, so $\beta$ is not
Pisot.  Finally $d=2$, $p=4$, and
$c_\beta=t_2+1=1<d$; case~\textup{(b)} of
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} yields injectivity and
$\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
\end{proof}

The cubic calculation below shows that the exact modulus-$Q_m$ survival depth
is already unbounded in algebraic degree three.

\input{sections/05c_secondary_comparisons}

\section{Concluding remarks}\label{sec:discussion}

The first conclusion is a complete finite-type classification for the cyclic
fixed-length recodings attached to quadratic Pisot bases.  Theorem
\ref{thm:full-quadratic-pisot-threshold} proves that the nontrivial threshold
is always two or three, and identifies exactly the two families attaining
three.  Above threshold the classification is not merely asymptotic: the
same theorem gives the exact onset of the block bijection, Theorem
\ref{thm:fischer-cover-stabilized-image}
identifies the right Fischer cover, and the corresponding Markov order is
sharp.  In particular,
\[
 |\mathcal L_n(Y_{\beta,m})|
 =(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+1)^{n+m-1}
 \qquad(n\ge m\ge m_*(\beta)).
\]
The canonical Parry--Bertrand rank used to define the recoding belongs to
the established numeration theory.  The new step is its cyclic reduction at
fixed word length and the parameter-uniform overlap analysis that yields
these exact dynamical invariants.

The second conclusion concerns the simple-Parry arithmetic difference graph.
Pair graphs, fiber products, directed-cycle tests for injectivity, and finite
inverse-window decisions are classical tools for one-dimensional local codes
and tessellation automata.  Theorem
\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} applies that decision after passing
to a coordinatewise-difference quotient adapted to the Parry recurrence.
What is specific here is the proof that this quotient captures every
collision, the longest-path formula for the exact memory-zero inverse
anticipation, the explicit bounds, and the unique-zero-predecessor mechanism
that turns the classical decision into a future-only inverse in this setting.
Corollary~\ref{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness} therefore leaves no
intermediate regime between a finite future-only inverse and a bounded
periodic collision certificate.  The aperture-two classification in
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} and the quadratic aperture
trichotomy give the promised special compression and dichotomy.

The third conclusion is that algebraic degree does not bound this decoding
depth.  For Bassino's cubic family
\[
 x^3-(n+2)x^2+2nx-n,
\]
Theorem~\ref{thm:cubic-unbounded-causal-depth} proves exact memory-zero
inverse anticipation $n-2$, equivalently future-only inverse length $n-1$, at aperture
$n-1$.  Thus the depth is unbounded already among degree-three simple-Parry
systems and injective apertures.  The point is the exact sliding collision
depth for this established family, not the existence of its Parry expansion.

Two boundaries of the classification remain explicit.  The cyclic
language-rank construction does not identify numerical $\beta$-values, and
it does not treat signed or redundant alphabets, non-greedy transformations,
higher-degree non-simple-Parry bases, or general Pisot substitutions; these
interfaces are recorded in Remark~\ref{rem:quadratic-open-interface}.  In the
quadratic negative-conjugate chamber, aperture three has exact future-only inverse length
two, whereas for $m\ge4$ the present bounds are
$2\le\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)\le m$.  Remark
\ref{rem:negative-two-window-open-interface} isolates the additional
parameter-uniform carry classification needed to close that narrower gap.

The entropy, Blackwell, pressure, fiber, discrepancy, bounded-multiple, and
zeta calculations are supplementary consequences.
They provide comparisons and invariant checks, but none enters the
separation estimate, the Fischer-cover argument, the simple-Parry
two-window exclusion, or the cubic exhaustion.  The main proof chain is the
three-part dynamical classification above.

\section{Classical pair graphs and simple-Parry arithmetic quotients}
\label{sec:simple-parry-causal-obstructions}

The pair-graph decision used in this section is classical.  Decision
procedures for injectivity and surjectivity of one-dimensional local maps go
back to tessellation automata and cellular automata
\cite{AmorosoPatt1972,Richardson1972}.  Nasu's bundle graphs encode two input
paths with a common image, and Head formulates injectivity as unambiguity of a
labeled finite automaton \cite{Nasu1977,Head1989}.  In symbolic dynamics this
is the familiar fiber-product or pair-graph construction
\cite{LindMarcus1995,Nasu1995}.  A reachable off-diagonal directed cycle,
its periodic repetition, and the existence of some finite sliding-block
inverse in the injective case all belong to that tradition.  Quantitative
decoder windows likewise have an established theory
\cite{Ashley1988,Ashley1991,Ashley1996}.

What is specific here is the quotient of the full input-pair graph by the
coordinatewise difference: for cyclic language-rank recodings it closes on
the finite state set $\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  This arithmetic quotient gives the
exact memory-zero anticipation as a longest survival time, a concrete state
bound, and, for simple-Parry ranks, a unique-zero-predecessor property that
turns a general two-sided inverse into a future-only inverse.  The aperture
trichotomy and the exact quadratic and cubic depths are computations in this
quotient, not new pair-graph principles.  Throughout, the congruences concern
integer language ranks, not equality of numerical $\beta$-values.

Recall that Pisot bases have eventually periodic greedy expansions of one
\cite{Schmidt1980}, whereas the finite expansion required below is the
simple-Parry hypothesis.  The collision results through
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} do not require the base to be
Pisot.  Parry admissibility and the associated Bertrand numeration are standard
\cite{Parry1960,CharlierCisterninoStipulanti2022}; the expansions of one for
cubic Pisot numbers were classified by Bassino \cite{Bassino2002}.  We record
the data needed
to distinguish these published inputs from the new sliding-congruence
statement.

Let $\beta\notin\mathbb Z$ be a simple-Parry number with
\[
d_\beta(1)=t_1t_2\cdots t_p0^\infty,
\qquad t_1=d=\lfloor\beta\rfloor,
\qquad t_p>0,
\]
where $p\ge2$ and $0\le t_i\le d$.  The language used below is the canonical
Parry language determined by
$d_\beta^*(1)=(t_1\cdots t_{p-1}(t_p-1))^\infty$, not the noncanonical
Bertrand system generated directly by the finite word $d_\beta(1)$.
Bertrand--Mathis introduced the positional framework
\cite{BertrandMathis1989}; the canonical/noncanonical distinction in the
simple-Parry case is part of the correction and classification in
\cite{CharlierCisterninoStipulanti2022}.  Put
\[
P_\beta(z)=z^p-t_1z^{p-1}-\cdots-t_p,
\qquad
\mathcal A_d=\{0,1,\ldots,d\},
\qquad
\Delta_d=[-d,d]\cap\mathbb Z.
\]
The raw system is the full shift $\mathcal A_d^{\mathbb Z}$, so $\Delta_d$
is exactly its digit-difference alphabet.  Let $Q_n$ be the number of legal
length-$n$ words.  In the low-to-high convention used throughout this
article, the established Parry--Bertrand rank data are
\begin{align}
Q_0&=1,\nonumber\\
Q_n&=1+\sum_{i=1}^n t_iQ_{n-i} &&(1\le n<p),\label{eq:simple-parry-Q}\\
Q_n&=\sum_{i=1}^p t_iQ_{n-i} &&(n\ge p),\nonumber
\end{align}
and
\[
\Rank_{\beta,m}(w)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}w_jQ_j
\]
maps the legal length-$m$ words, in colexicographic order, bijectively onto
$\{0,\ldots,Q_m-1\}$.  We therefore define $\Fold_{\beta,m}$ and
$\Phi_{\beta,m}$ by the same rank reduction modulo $Q_m$ as in
Definition~\ref{def:quadratic-parry-fold}.

For $m\ge2$, define
\begin{equation}
\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
=\min\{L\ge1:a_t=\psi(y_t,\ldots,y_{t+L-1})
\text{ for every }y=\Phi_{\beta,m}(a)\},
\label{eq:simple-parry-causal-length}
\end{equation}
with value $\infty$ if no such $L$ exists.  Thus
$\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)-1$ is the least anticipation of an inverse
sliding-block code constrained to have memory zero.  We do not use
``right-closing delay'' as a synonym: the latter belongs to general
factor-map theory, while \eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-length} fixes a
one-sided convention and asks for its exact optimum.  Also set
\[
\mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)
=\min\{\deg H:0\ne H\in(P_\beta),\ H(0)\ne0,
\ |[z^j]H|\le d\text{ for all }j\}.
\]

\begin{proposition}[Degeneration of the bounded-multiple order]
\label{prop:simple-parry-bounded-order}
Under the preceding simple-Parry hypotheses,
\[
\mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)=p.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The coefficient vector of $P_\beta$ consists of the leading coefficient
$1$ and the coefficients $-t_1,\ldots,-t_p$.  It lies in $\Delta_d$, and its
constant coefficient $-t_p$ is nonzero.  Hence
$\mathfrak a(P_\beta,d)\le p$.  Conversely, every nonzero member of the
principal ideal $(P_\beta)\subset\mathbb Z[z]$ has degree at least
$\deg P_\beta=p$.  This proves equality.
\end{proof}

For $L\ge1$, introduce the bounded sliding-congruence obstruction
\begin{equation}
\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}
=\left\{e\in\Delta_d^{m+L-1}:
e_0\ne0,\quad
\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Q_je_{t+j}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}
\ (0\le t<L)\right\}.
\label{eq:bad-sliding-congruence-set}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, let $T_{m,L}$ be the $L$-row banded Toeplitz matrix obtained by
placing the kernel $(Q_0,\ldots,Q_{m-1})$ at offsets
$t=0,\ldots,L-1$.  Then the last condition is
$T_{m,L}e\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}$.

\subsection{The classical decision in the arithmetic difference quotient}

\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic quotient of the classical pair-graph decision]
\label{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}
Apply the classical pair-graph construction to the cyclic rank recoding of a
nonintegral simple-Parry number.  For every $m\ge2$, its coordinatewise
difference quotient satisfies
\begin{equation}
\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
=\min\{L\ge1:\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}=\varnothing\},
\label{eq:exact-causal-obstruction}
\end{equation}
where the minimum is $\infty$ when all the obstruction sets are nonempty.

More explicitly, let $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ be the directed graph with vertex set
$V=\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  There is an edge from
$v=(v_0,\ldots,v_{m-2})$ to $w=(w_0,\ldots,w_{m-2})$ precisely when
\[
w_i=v_{i+1}\quad(0\le i<m-2)
\]
and
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=0}^{m-2}Q_jv_j+Q_{m-1}w_{m-2}
\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
\label{eq:collision-graph-edge}
\end{equation}
If $B=\{v\in V:v_0\ne0\}$, then
\begin{equation}
\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
=1+\sup\{\text{number of edges in a directed path starting in }B\}.
\label{eq:collision-graph-longest-path}
\end{equation}
The supremum is infinite exactly when a directed cycle is reachable from
$B$.  Furthermore,
\begin{equation}
\Phi_{\beta,m}\text{ is injective}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\Gamma_{\beta,m}\text{ has no bi-infinite path other than the zero path}.
\label{eq:collision-graph-injectivity}
\end{equation}
If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, then its future-only inverse length is
finite exactly when the zero vertex is not reachable from $B$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $u,v\in\mathcal A_d^{m+L-1}$ and put $e_i=u_i-v_i$.  Since the legal
rank is a bijection, the $t$th output symbols of the two blocks are equal if
and only if their raw ranks agree modulo $Q_m$, namely if and only if
\[
\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Q_je_{t+j}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
\]
Moreover, $u_0\ne v_0$ if and only if $e_0\ne0$.  Thus every pair of raw
blocks whose first symbols remain ambiguous after $L$ outputs determines an
element of $\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$.

Conversely, for any $e\in\Delta_d^{m+L-1}$ define coordinatewise
\[
u_i=\max\{e_i,0\},\qquad v_i=\max\{-e_i,0\}.
\]
Then $u_i,v_i\in\mathcal A_d$, $u_i-v_i=e_i$, and both finite blocks extend
arbitrarily to points of the full shift.  Hence every element of
$\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$ is realized by an actual output collision with
distinct first symbols.  If the obstruction set is empty, the first raw
symbol is constant on every fiber over an occurring length-$L$ output word;
the resulting function on this finite output language is the decoder $\psi$
in \eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-length}.  This proves
\eqref{eq:exact-causal-obstruction}.

A length-$L$ path in $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ beginning at
$(e_0,\ldots,e_{m-2})\in B$ is exactly a vector
$e\in\mathcal N_{\beta,m,L}$: each edge appends one coordinate and imposes
one of the $L$ congruences.  Formula
\eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} follows.  Since the graph is finite,
arbitrarily long such paths exist exactly when a directed cycle is reachable
from $B$.

A bi-infinite difference sequence labels a bi-infinite path in the graph if
and only if all corresponding output ranks agree.  Every coordinate of that
difference sequence is realized by two raw digits by the positive--negative
part construction above.  Therefore a nonzero bi-infinite graph path is
equivalent to a pair of distinct points with the same image, proving
\eqref{eq:collision-graph-injectivity}.

Finally assume injectivity.  No directed cycle containing a nonzero vertex
can then occur, because its periodic repetition would be a nonzero
bi-infinite path.  The zero loop is consequently the only cycle reachable
from $B$ that is compatible with injectivity.  Thus paths from $B$ have
unbounded length exactly when they reach the zero vertex.  Combining this
with \eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Simple-Parry consequence of the classical decision]
\label{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}
Under the preceding hypotheses, for every $m\ge2$ the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  \item $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective;
  \item $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)<\infty$;
  \item no directed cycle of $\Gamma_{\beta,m}$ is reachable from $B$.
\end{enumerate}
If these conditions hold, then
\begin{equation}
1\le \ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,m)
\le (2d+1)^{m-1}-1.
\label{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}
\end{equation}
If they fail, there are distinct periodic raw points with the same image and
with common period at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$.  Thus the classical finite
forward cycle search specializes to the arithmetic difference quotient
above.  Every failure has a purely periodic certificate with the displayed
explicit bound.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The recurrence \eqref{eq:simple-parry-Q} gives $Q_1=d+1$ and shows
inductively that $Q_n>Q_{n-1}$: before the terminal index there is the
additional summand $1$, while at and after that index the positive term
$t_pQ_{n-p}$ accompanies $dQ_{n-1}$.  In particular, $Q_m>d$.

Let $\mathbf0=(0,\ldots,0)\in\Delta_d^{m-1}$.  If an edge
$v\to\mathbf0$ exists, the overlap conditions give
$v_1=\cdots=v_{m-2}=0$, and the edge congruence reduces to
$v_0\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}$.  Since $|v_0|\le d<Q_m$, necessarily
$v=\mathbf0$.  Hence the zero vertex has the zero vertex as its unique
predecessor and is not reachable from $B$.

If $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is injective, the last assertion of
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} now gives a finite
future-only inverse.  The converse is immediate because a decoder recovers every raw
coordinate.  A directed cycle reachable from $B$ cannot contain
$\mathbf0$; repeating its first-coordinate labels gives a nonzero periodic
difference sequence and hence, by taking positive and negative parts, two
distinct periodic raw points with the same image.  Conversely, from any
nonzero bi-infinite collision path, shift to a vertex in $B$.  Its forward
path avoids $\mathbf0$ and therefore repeats among the
$(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ nonzero vertices, producing a reachable directed cycle.
This proves all three equivalences and the periodic certificate, whose cycle
may be chosen simple.

In the injective case the forward subgraph reachable from $B$ is acyclic and
contains at most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-1$ vertices.  A directed path in it has at
most $(2d+1)^{m-1}-2$ edges.  Formula
\eqref{eq:collision-graph-longest-path} then gives
\eqref{eq:simple-parry-causal-state-bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Complete aperture-two trichotomy]
\label{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two}
Set
\[
c_\beta=t_2+\ind_{\{p\ge3\}}.
\]
Then $1\le c_\beta\le d+1$ and
\[
Q_2=d(d+1)+c_\beta.
\]
Exactly one of the following alternatives holds.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
  \item If $c_\beta=d+1$, then $\Fold_{\beta,2}$ is the identity on
  $\mathcal A_d^2$ and $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=1$.
  \item If $1\le c_\beta<d$, then $\Phi_{\beta,2}$ is injective and
  $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
  \item If $c_\beta=d$, then $\Phi_{\beta,2}$ is noninjective.  Its sole
  nonsingleton path-space fiber is
  \[
  \Phi_{\beta,2}(0^{\mathbb Z})
  =\Phi_{\beta,2}(d^{\mathbb Z})
  =((0,0))^{\mathbb Z},
  \]
  and $\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
For $p=2$ the three cases are determined by $c_\beta=t_2$; for
$p\ge3$ they are determined by $c_\beta=t_2+1$.  In particular, a binary
simple-Parry word of order at least three has an identity aperture-two fold
when $t_2=1$ and exactly the displayed constant branch pair when $t_2=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $q=d+1$ and $M=Q_2=dq+c_\beta$.  The formula for $Q_2$ follows directly
from \eqref{eq:simple-parry-Q}, with the boundary summand $1$ present exactly
when $2<p$.  Since $t_2\ge1$ when $p=2$, whereas $0\le t_2\le d$ when
$p\ge3$, the stated range for $c_\beta$ follows.

An edge of $\Gamma_{\beta,2}$ is a pair $(e,f)\in\Delta_d^2$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
e+qf\equiv0\pmod M.
\label{eq:aperture-two-edge-congruence}
\end{equation}
The left side has modulus at most
$d(1+q)=q^2-1<2M$, so it equals $-M$, $0$, or $M$.  If it is zero, then
$q>|e|$ forces $(e,f)=(0,0)$.  If it is $M$, the bound $e\le d$ forces
$f=d$, because $f\le d-1$ would give
$M-qf\ge q+c_\beta>d$; hence $(e,f)=(c_\beta,d)$, which is admissible
exactly when $c_\beta\le d$.  The case $-M$ is its negative.  Thus the full
edge set is
\[
\{(0,0)\}\quad(c_\beta=d+1),
\qquad
\{(0,0),\ \pm(c_\beta,d)\}\quad(c_\beta\le d).
\]

When $c_\beta=d+1$, one has $Q_2=q^2=|\mathcal A_d^2|$, so every raw
length-two word is legal and cyclic reduction does nothing.  This proves
(a).  If $c_\beta<d$, the two nonzero edges cannot be followed by another
edge, because their terminal vertices are $\pm d$, not $\pm c_\beta$.
There is a one-output collision but no two-output collision, so
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision} gives (b).  Finally, when
$c_\beta=d$, the two nonzero edges are the loops $(d,d)$ and $(-d,-d)$.
They give precisely the constant difference sequences $\pm d^{\mathbb Z}$.
The only raw realization of either is the pair
$\{0^{\mathbb Z},d^{\mathbb Z}\}$, and the common residue is zero in every
window.  No other nonzero bi-infinite path exists, proving (c) and the fiber
classification.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Pisot-free scope is strict]
\label{prop:nonpisot-simple-parry-scope}
Proposition~\ref{prop:simple-parry-bounded-order},
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-causal-collision}, Corollary
\ref{cor:simple-parry-causal-completeness}, and Theorem
\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} hold for every nonintegral simple-Parry
number; no Pisot hypothesis is required.  This strictly enlarges their scope.
Indeed, let $\beta$ be the positive root of
\begin{equation}
x^4-2x^3-2=0.
\label{eq:nonpisot-simple-parry-witness}
\end{equation}
Then
\[
d_\beta(1)=2002\,0^\infty,
\]
$\beta$ is not Pisot, and the aperture-two cyclic rank fold is injective with
$\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The recurrence for $Q_n$, the consecutive colex-rank formula, and Parry
admissibility used above hold for every simple-Parry number
\cite{Parry1960,CharlierCisterninoStipulanti2022}.  The proofs of the four
listed results subsequently use only these data, the full raw alphabet
$\{0,\ldots,d\}$, integer congruences modulo $Q_m$, and finite directed
graphs.  No estimate on a Galois conjugate occurs.  This proves the claimed
hypothesis removal.

It remains to show that the enlargement is strict.  The polynomial in
\eqref{eq:nonpisot-simple-parry-witness} is Eisenstein at $2$, hence
irreducible.  Its positive root satisfies
\[
2<\beta<\frac{11}{5},
\]
because the polynomial takes the values $-2$ and $81/625$ at the two
endpoints.  Its defining equation gives
\[
1=2\beta^{-1}+2\beta^{-4}.
\]
The proper suffixes of $2002\,0^\infty$ are all lexicographically smaller
than $2002\,0^\infty$: the first two begin with $0$, while the third is
$2000^\infty<2002\,0^\infty$.  Parry's criterion therefore gives
$d_\beta(1)=2002\,0^\infty$.

Write the negative real root as $-u$.  The function
$g(u)=u^4+2u^3-2$ is strictly increasing for $u>0$, with $g(0)<0<g(1)$,
so $0<u<1$.  To count all real roots, put
$f(x)=x^4-2x^3-2$.  Its derivative is
\[
f'(x)=2x^2(2x-3).
\]
Thus $f$ is strictly decreasing on $(-\infty,3/2)$ apart from the stationary
flattening at $0$, which does not change monotonicity, and is strictly
increasing on $(3/2,\infty)$.  Since $f(x)\to+\infty$ as
$x\to-\infty$, $f(0)=-2$, and $f(x)\to+\infty$ as $x\to+\infty$, there is
exactly one negative real root and exactly one positive real root.  The two
remaining roots are therefore a nonreal conjugate pair $z,\overline z$.
Moreover,
\[
\beta^4 g(2/\beta)
=4(3+4\beta-\beta^3)>0,
\]
where the last inequality follows from
$\beta<11/5$ and
$3+4\beta-\beta^3>11-(11/5)^3=44/125$.
Thus $u<2/\beta$.  The product of the four roots is $-2$, whence
\[
|z|^2=\frac{2}{\beta u}>1.
\]
Irreducibility makes $z$ a Galois conjugate of $\beta$, so $\beta$ is not
Pisot.  Finally $d=2$, $p=4$, and
$c_\beta=t_2+1=1<d$; case~\textup{(b)} of
Theorem~\ref{thm:simple-parry-aperture-two} yields injectivity and
$\ell_{\rm cau}(\beta,2)=2$.
\end{proof}

The cubic calculation below shows that the exact modulus-$Q_m$ survival depth
is already unbounded in algebraic degree three.


\input{sections/05c_bassino_cubic_depth}
\ifdefined\IncludeSupplementaryConsequences
\input{sections/05c_secondary_comparisons}
\fi


 succeeded in 204ms:
\subsection{The bounded sliding-congruence lemma}

The separation estimate used in the sliding argument is isolated first.  In
particular, the integer multiple and the error term are both retained.

\begin{lemma}[Nearest-multiple separation]
\label{lem:nearest-multiple-separation}
Let $Q_0,Q_1,\ldots$ satisfy either \eqref{eq:qminus} or \eqref{eq:qplus},
write $s=1$ or $s=-1$ accordingly, and put
\[
d=\begin{cases}a,&s=1,\\a-1,&s=-1.\end{cases}
\]
Then, for $r\ge4$ and $1\le |e|\le d$,
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{dist}(beQ_{r-1},Q_r\mathbb Z)\ge Q_{r-2}.
\label{eq:quadratic-separation}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Distance is unchanged by replacing $e$ with $-e$, so assume $1\le e\le d$.
Set
\[
R_r:=\frac{Q_{r-1}}{Q_{r-2}}.
\]
The recurrence gives $R_{r+1}=a+sb/R_r$.  Its initial values and the parameter
ranges imply, by induction,
\begin{align}
a+\frac{b}{a+1}&\le R_r<a+\frac ba &&(s=1,\ r\ge3),
\label{eq:ratio-plus}\\
a-\frac{b}{a-1}&<R_r\le a-\frac ba &&(s=-1,\ r\ge3).
\label{eq:ratio-minus}
\end{align}
For completeness, in the positive-recurrence case the map
$x\mapsto a+b/x$ reverses order, sends
$[a+b/(a+1),a+b/a)$ into itself, and $R_3=a+b/(a+1)$.
In the negative-recurrence case $x\mapsto a-b/x$ preserves order, the interval
in \eqref{eq:ratio-minus} lies in $(a-1,a)$, and $R_3=a-b/a$.
These observations prove the two inductions, including the stated strict
endpoints.

We shall also use the following sharpened bounds, obtained by applying the
same recurrence once more:
\begin{align*}
L_+&:=a+\frac{b}{a+b/(a+b/(a+1))},&
U_+&:=a+\frac{b}{a+b/(a+1)},\\
L_-&:=a-\frac{b}{a-1},&
U_-&:=a-\frac{b}{a-b/a}.
\end{align*}
Thus
\begin{equation}
L_+\le R_r\le U_+\quad(s=1,r\ge4),
\qquad
L_-<R_r\le U_-\quad(s=-1,r\ge4).
\label{eq:sharpened-ratio-bounds}
\end{equation}
The first assertion follows because the order-reversing map interchanges the
endpoints $R_4=U_+$ and $R_5=L_+$ and maps $[L_+,U_+]$ into itself.  The
second follows because $R_3=a-b/a$, $R_4=U_-$, and the increasing map sends
$(L_-,U_-]$ into itself.

Choose a nearest integer $k$ to
\[
z:=\frac{beQ_{r-1}}{Q_r}=\frac{be}{R_{r+1}}.
\]
The lower bounds $R_{r+1}>a$ for $s=1$ and $R_{r+1}>a-1$ for $s=-1$ give
\[
0<z<b.
\]
Indeed $e\le a$ in the first case and $e\le a-1$ in the second.  Hence every
nearest integer satisfies
\begin{equation}
0\le k\le b.
\label{eq:nearest-k-bound}
\end{equation}
Put $h:=be-ak$.  The exact error, with no omitted remainder, is
\begin{align}
E&:=beQ_{r-1}-kQ_r\notag\\
 &=(be-ak)Q_{r-1}-skbQ_{r-2}
   =(hR_r-skb)Q_{r-2}.
\label{eq:nearest-error}
\end{align}

\paragraph{The positive recurrence.}
Put
\[
S:=a^2+a+b,
\qquad
T:=a^3+a^2+2ab+b=aS+b(a+1).
\]
Both denominators are positive for every $a\ge b\ge1$, and reduction of the
nested fractions gives
\begin{equation}
U_+-a=\frac{b(a+1)}S,
\qquad
L_+-a=\frac{bS}T.
\label{eq:positive-offsets}
\end{equation}
We first record the bounded integer exclusion for the cancelling sign.  For
$1\le e\le a$, $0\le k\le b$, and $h=be-ak$,
\begin{equation}
h>0\Longrightarrow
  hL_+-kb\ge1\quad\hbox{or}\quad kb-hU_+\ge1.
\label{eq:endpoint-plus}
\end{equation}
Write $j=kb-ah$.  Since $h=be-ak$, solving for $(e,k)$ gives
\[
k=\frac{ah+j}{b},\qquad
e=\frac{(a^2+b)h+aj}{b^2}.
\]
Thus the two displayed numerators are divisible by $b$ and $b^2$,
respectively.  We give the remaining Euclidean divisions explicitly.  Write
\[
be=aq+r,\qquad qb=ap+t,\qquad 0\le r,t<a.
\]
Because $be\le ab$, one has $0\le q\le b$; if $r>0$, then $q\le b-1$.
These statements use no coprimality of $a$ and $b$.
If $k\le q-1$, then $h\ge a+r$ and
\[
hL_+-kb>(a+r)a-(b-1)b
\ge a^2-b(b-1)\ge a\ge1.
\]
If $k\ge q+1$, then $h<0$, so this case is not in the hypothesis of
\eqref{eq:endpoint-plus}.  Hence only $k=q$ remains, and then $h=r$.
Now $j=a(p-r)+t$.  The cases $p<r$ and $p>r$ give, respectively,
$hL_+-kb\ge1$ and $kb-hU_+\ge1$.  Explicitly, when $p<r$,
\[
rL_+-qb=a(r-p)-t+r(L_+-a)>a-t\ge1,
\]
whereas, when $p>r$, the bounds $0<U_+-a<b/a$ and $r<a$ give
\[
qb-rU_+=a(p-r)+t-r(U_+-a)
>a-\frac{rb}{a}\ge a-b+\frac ba\ge1.
\]
When $p=r$, put $w=rb-at$.  The two division identities give
\begin{equation}
b^2e=r(a^2+b)+at,\qquad w=r(a^2+2b)-b^2e.
\label{eq:plus-terminal-divisions}
\end{equation}
The integer $w$ is nonzero.  To verify this without a hidden coprimality
assumption, suppose $w=0$ and write $a=gA$, $b=gB$ with
$\gcd(A,B)=1$.  From $at=rb$ one obtains $r=AR$ and $t=BR$ with
$0<R<g$.  Reducing $be=aq+r$ and $qb=ar+t$ modulo $g$ gives
$g\mid AR$ and $g\mid BR$.  Bezout's identity gives $g\mid R$, a
contradiction.

It remains to audit the signs of $w$.  Make the third Euclidean division
\begin{equation}
rb=au+v,\qquad 0\le v<a.
\label{eq:positive-third-division}
\end{equation}
Thus $w=a(u-t)+v$.  Reversing the three divisions
\[
r=be-aq,\qquad t=qb-ar,\qquad v=rb-au
\]
and retaining the ranges $1\le e\le a$, $0\le q\le b$, and $0<r<a$
gives the complete boundary sign audit
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
\text{sign}&\text{quotient case}&\text{remainder conclusion}\\ \hline
w>0&u-t\ge2&\text{no extra inequality}\cr
w>0&u-t=1&av\ge b(t+1)\cr
w<0&t-u\ge2&\text{no extra inequality}\cr
w<0&t-u=1&b(t-1)\ge v(a+1).
\end{array}
\label{eq:positive-terminal-sign-table}
\end{equation}
No other quotient case has the indicated sign.  Here is the integer check
behind the two boundary rows.  Starting with $u=t+1$, or with $u=t-1$,
substitute successively in the reverse divisions
\[
r=\frac{au+v}{b},\qquad
q=\frac{ar+t}{b},\qquad
e=\frac{aq+r}{b}.
\]
After multiplication by the positive denominators $b,b^2,b^3$, respectively,
the failed inequalities $av<b(t+1)$ and $b(t-1)<v(a+1)$ leave, in order,
either $r\ge a$, $q>b$, or a nonintegral final quotient.  These contradict
the displayed ranges.  If equality is met before the last division, write
$a=gA$, $b=gB$ with $(A,B)=1$; the residual quotient is strictly between
$0$ and $g$, while the three divisibilities and Bezout's identity force it
to be divisible by $g$.  This is the same non-coprime endpoint exclusion as
for $w=0$, and shows that the four rows are exhaustive.

We now clear the two endpoint inequalities line by line.  Define
\begin{align*}
\Delta_L&:=rbS-(t+1)T
          =T\{r(L_+-a)-t-1\},\\
\Delta_U&:=tS-rb(a+1)-S
          =S\{t-r(U_+-a)-1\}.
\end{align*}
Using $T=aS+b(a+1)$ and $w=a(u-t)+v$ gives the exact identities
\begin{align}
\Delta_L&=wS-T-tb(a+1),
\label{eq:positive-terminal-left-clear}\\
\Delta_U&=tb+(-w)(a+1)-S.
\label{eq:positive-terminal-right-clear}
\end{align}
If $w>0$ and $u-t\ge2$, then $w\ge2a$ and $t\le a-1$, so
\[
\Delta_L
\ge2aS-T-(a-1)b(a+1)
=a^2(a-b+1)>0.
\]
If $w>0$ and $u-t=1$, then the second row of
\eqref{eq:positive-terminal-sign-table} and $S>a(a+1)$ give
\[
\Delta_L=vS-b(a+1)(t+1)\ge0.
\]
Thus $r(L_+-a)-t\ge1$ in both positive-$w$ subcases.

If $w<0$ and $t-u\ge2$, then $-w\ge2a-(a-1)=a+1$, whence
\[
\Delta_U\ge(a+1)^2-S=a+1-b\ge1.
\]
If $w<0$ and $t-u=1$, the last row of the sign table gives exactly
\[
\Delta_U=b(t-1)-v(a+1)\ge0.
\]
Thus $t-r(U_+-a)\ge1$ in both negative-$w$ subcases.  Since $w\ne0$,
the branches exhaust $p=r$ and prove \eqref{eq:endpoint-plus}.

\paragraph{The negative recurrence.}
The cancelling sign here has a norm proof.  Let $\beta$ and $\alpha$ be the
roots of $x^2-ax+b$, with $0<\alpha<1<\beta$, and put
$g=-h=ak-be>0$.  The closed formula for the recurrence gives
\[
R_r=\frac{\beta^r-\alpha^r}{\beta^{r-1}-\alpha^{r-1}}>\beta,
\]
while \eqref{eq:sharpened-ratio-bounds} gives $R_r\le U_-$.  Consider the
nonzero integral norm
\[
\nu:=k^2-aek+be^2=(k-e\alpha)(k-e\beta).
\]
Here $k\le b\le a-2<\beta\le e\beta$, so the second factor is negative.
Neither factor vanishes because $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are irrational and
$e>0$.  Hence $\nu\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$.

If $\nu<0$, then $k-e\alpha>0$, and $b=\alpha\beta$ and
$\beta^2=a\beta-b$ give
\[
g\beta-kb=\beta^2(k-e\alpha)
=\frac{\beta^2|\nu|}{e\beta-k}>1;
\]
indeed its denominator is positive and
\[
0<e\beta-k<e\beta\le(a-1)\beta<\beta^2.
\]
This remains strict at $e=a-1$.  Since $R_r>\beta$, it follows that
$gR_r-kb>1$.

Suppose $\nu>0$ and set $\delta=e\alpha-k>0$.  Then
\[
g=e\alpha^2-a\delta>0,
\qquad
\nu=\delta\{e(\beta-\alpha)+\delta\}\ge1.
\]
In particular
$0<\delta<e\alpha^2/a\le(a-1)\alpha^2/a<1$.  Put $D=a^2-b$ and
$H=(a-1)(\beta-\alpha)+1$.  Since $e\le a-1$, the norm identity gives
$\delta>H^{-1}$.  To see the strict endpoint, use $0<\delta<1$ to obtain
\[
e(\beta-\alpha)+\delta
<(a-1)(\beta-\alpha)+1=H;
\]
then $1\le\nu=\delta\{e(\beta-\alpha)+\delta\}$ gives
$\delta>H^{-1}$.  Every denominator used below is positive:
\[
D=a^2-b\ge a^2-a+2>0,
\qquad H>1,
\qquad \alpha>0,\quad \beta>0.
\]
Moreover
\[
\alpha-\frac{ab}{D}=\frac{\alpha^3}{D},
\]
and therefore
\begin{align*}
kb-gU_-
&=\beta^2\delta-\frac{g\alpha^3}{D}\\
&=\delta\left(\beta^2+\frac{a\alpha^3}{D}\right)
  -\frac{e\alpha^5}{D}\\
&>\frac{\beta^2}{H}-\frac{(a-1)\alpha^5}{D}>1.
\end{align*}
For the final inequality, write $a=\alpha+\beta$ and $b=\alpha\beta$.
Direct expansion gives
\[
D-H=2\alpha^2+\alpha(\beta-1)+(\beta-1)>0
\]
and
\[
\beta^2-H=\beta-1-\alpha(1-\alpha)>(a-1)\alpha^5.
\]
Indeed $\alpha^5<\alpha$, while $b\le a-2$ is equivalent to
$\beta(1-\alpha)\ge2-\alpha>1$.  Consequently
\begin{align*}
\beta^2-H
&=\beta-1-\alpha(1-\alpha)\\
&=(a-1)\alpha+\{\beta(1-\alpha)-1\}\\
&>(a-1)\alpha>(a-1)\alpha^5.
\end{align*}
At the boundary $b=a-2$ one has
$\beta(1-\alpha)=2-\alpha>1$, so the first strict margin is not lost.
Since $D>H>0$, division gives
\[
\frac{\beta^2-H}{H}
>\frac{(a-1)\alpha^5}{H}
>\frac{(a-1)\alpha^5}{D}.
\]
This is equivalent to
$\beta^2/H-(a-1)\alpha^5/D>1$, and completes every step in the chain
to $kb-gU_->1$, including $e=a-1$.  Since $R_r\le U_-$, this case gives
$kb-gR_r>1$.

We now cover all signs explicitly.  If $s=1$ and $h<0$, then
\[
|hR_r-kb|=(-h)R_r+kb>1.
\]
If $h=0$, then $k\ge1$ (because $e\ge1$), and
$|hR_r-kb|=kb\ge1$.  If $h>0$, the two alternatives in
\eqref{eq:endpoint-plus}, together with
$L_+\le R_r\le U_+$, give $|hR_r-kb|\ge1$.

If $s=-1$ and $h>0$, then $hR_r+kb>1$.  If $h=0$, again $k\ge1$ and the
modulus is $kb\ge1$.  The case $h<0$ was proved directly above.
Thus in both chambers
\[
|hR_r-skb|\ge1.
\]

\paragraph{Closure of the distance estimate.}
For the nearest integer $k$ chosen above, \eqref{eq:nearest-error} is the
exact identity
\[
E=beQ_{r-1}-kQ_r=(hR_r-skb)Q_{r-2}.
\]
Since $Q_{r-2}>0$, the chamberwise estimate gives $|E|\ge Q_{r-2}$.
Finally, $kQ_r\in Q_r\mathbb Z$ and $k$ was chosen nearest to
$beQ_{r-1}/Q_r$, so
\[
\operatorname{dist}(beQ_{r-1},Q_r\mathbb Z)
=|beQ_{r-1}-kQ_r|=|E|\ge Q_{r-2}.
\]
This is \eqref{eq:quadratic-separation}.
\end{proof}

The following sliding lemma is the arithmetic core of the block decoder.

\begin{lemma}[Quadratic bounded sliding congruences]
\label{lem:quadratic-sliding-congruences}
Take either parameter chamber in
Lemma~\ref{lem:quadratic-pisot-chambers}, let $d=\lfloor\beta\rfloor$, and
let $(Q_j)$ be \eqref{eq:qminus} or \eqref{eq:qplus}.  If $m\ge3$ and
$e_0,\ldots,e_{2m-2}\in[-d,d]\cap\mathbb Z$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Q_j e_{t+j}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}
\qquad(0\le t<m),\label{eq:sliding-kernel}
\end{equation}
then $e_0=\cdots=e_{2m-2}=0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $s=1$ in the negative chamber and $s=-1$ in the positive chamber, so
$Q_{j+2}=aQ_{j+1}+sbQ_j$, and put
$\delta=Q_1-a$; hence $(d,\delta)=(a,1)$ for $s=1$ and
$(d,\delta)=(a-1,0)$ for $s=-1$.  Subtracting $a$ times the congruence at
$t+1$ and $sb$ times that at $t+2$ from the congruence at $t$ cancels every
interior coefficient and gives
\begin{equation}
e_t+\delta e_{t+1}-sbQ_{m-1}e_{t+m+1}\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
\label{eq:quadratic-elimination}
\end{equation}

Suppose first that $m\ge4$.  The small part of
\eqref{eq:quadratic-elimination} has modulus at most
$d(1+\delta)$.  On the other hand,
\[
Q_{m-2}\ge Q_2=
\begin{cases}
a^2+a+b>2a=d(1+\delta),&s=1,\\
a^2-b>a-1=d(1+\delta),&s=-1.
\end{cases}
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:nearest-multiple-separation} therefore gives a strictly larger
distance whenever $e_{t+m+1}\ne0$.  Hence
$e_{t+m+1}=0$ for $0\le t\le m-3$.  Thus
$e_{m+1}=\cdots=e_{2m-2}=0$.  The final original congruence reduces to
\[
e_{m-1}+Q_1e_m\equiv0\pmod{Q_m}.
\]
Its left side has modulus at most $d(1+Q_1)<Q_m$ and $Q_1>d$, so both
digits vanish.  Back substitution in the preceding congruences then gives
$e_{m-2},\ldots,e_0=0$ successively.

It remains to check $m=3$, where endpoint equality in the separation bound
can occur.  Here each integer sum in \eqref{eq:sliding-kernel} has modulus
less than $2Q_3$, so it is $k_tQ_3$ with $k_t\in\{-1,0,1\}$.  Substituting
the three values of $t$ gives
\begin{equation}
e_t+Q_1e_{t+1}+Q_2e_{t+2}=k_tQ_3,
\qquad t=0,1,2.
\label{eq:quadratic-m3-equalities}
\end{equation}
We now eliminate the two conjugate signs separately.

Suppose first that $s=1$.  Thus $d=a$ and
\[
(Q_1,Q_2,Q_3)
=(a+1,a^2+a+b,a^3+a^2+2ab+b).
\]
Consider one equation $x+Q_1y+Q_2z=kQ_3$ occurring in
\eqref{eq:quadratic-m3-equalities}.  By symmetry it suffices to treat
$k=0,1$.  If $k=0$, then $|z|\ge2$ is impossible because
$2Q_2>a(1+Q_1)$.  The choice $z=0$ gives $x=y=0$, while $z=1$ gives
\[
x+b+(a+1)(y+a)=0.
\]
Since $y+a\ge0$ and $-(x+b)\le a-b<a+1$, this forces
$(x,y,z)=(-b,-a,1)$; the case $z=-1$ is its negative.  If $k=1$ and
$z\le a-1$, then
\[
x+Q_1y+Q_2z
\le a+aQ_1+(a-1)Q_2
=Q_3-\bigl(b(a+2)-a\bigr)<Q_3.
\]
Hence $z=a$, after which $x+(a+1)y=b(a+1)$ forces $(x,y)=(0,b)$.
Consequently every bounded solution of one equation is
\[
(x,y,z;k)\in
\{(0,0,0;0),\ \pm(b,a,-1;0),\ \pm(0,b,a;1)\}.
\]
The terminal pairs of these triples are
$(0,0)$, $\pm(a,-1)$, and $\pm(b,a)$.  If $e_4\ne0$ and the $t=2$
triple is $\pm(0,b,a)$, its initial pair $\pm(0,b)$ cannot be a terminal
pair of the $t=1$ triple.  If instead the $t=2$ triple is
$\pm(b,a,-1)$, the overlap forces the $t=1$ triple to be
$\pm(0,b,a)$, whose initial pair $\pm(0,b)$ cannot be a terminal pair of
the $t=0$ triple.  Both alternatives are impossible, and therefore
$e_4=0$.

Suppose next that $s=-1$.  Put $C=a-b$ and $d=a-1$; then
$2\le C\le d$ and
\[
(Q_1,Q_2,Q_3)=(a,a^2-b,a^3-2ab).
\]
For one equation $x+ay+Q_2z=kQ_3$, again it suffices to take $k=0,1$.
When $k=0$, the inequality $2Q_2>d(1+a)=a^2-1$ gives $|z|\le1$.
The choice $z=0$ gives $x=y=0$, whereas $z=1$ gives
\[
x-b+a(y+a)=0.
\]
Here $1\le y+a\le2a-1$ and $b-x\le b+a-1\le2a-3$, so necessarily
$y+a=1$ and $x=b-a=-C$; the case $z=-1$ is the negative solution.
For $k=1$, if $z\le a-2$, then
\[
x+ay+Q_2z
\le d(1+a)+(a-2)Q_2
=Q_3-\bigl(a(a-b)-2b+1\bigr)<Q_3,
\]
because $a-b\ge2$.  Thus $z=d$, and
\[
x+ay=a(a-b)-b=aC-b.
\]
Reduction modulo $a$, together with $|x|\le a-1$, leaves precisely
$(x,y)=(-b,C)$ and $(x,y)=(C,C-1)$.  Hence the complete list is
\[
(x,y,z;k)\in
\{(0,0,0;0),\ \pm(C,d,-1;0),\
  \ \pm(-b,C,d;1),\ \pm(C,C-1,d;1)\}.
\]
Its terminal pairs are $(0,0)$, $\pm(d,-1)$, $\pm(C,d)$, and
$\pm(C-1,d)$.  Since $2\le C\le d$, neither $\pm(-b,C)$ nor
$\pm(C,C-1)$ occurs in this list of terminal pairs.  Thus a $t=2$ triple
of either of the last two types cannot overlap a $t=1$ triple.  The only
remaining nonzero possibility is $\pm(C,d,-1)$; it forces the $t=1$
triple to be $\pm(-b,C,d)$, whose initial pair $\pm(-b,C)$ cannot overlap
a $t=0$ triple.  Therefore $e_4=0$ also in this chamber.

In either sign, every nonzero triple in the corresponding list has nonzero
last coordinate.  It follows successively from the equations at
$t=2,1,0$ that $e_2=e_3=e_4=0$, then $e_1=0$, and finally $e_0=0$.
Thus the kernel is trivial also at $m=3$.  The finite computation described in
the data-availability statement includes this case as regression testing; it
is not used as proof of the parameter-uniform statement.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sharp threshold and extremal bases}

\begin{theorem}[Exact quadratic-Pisot threshold]
\label{thm:full-quadratic-pisot-threshold}
Let $\beta$ be an arbitrary quadratic Pisot number and use exactly the
greedy language, Parry data, rank, cyclic modulus and fold of
Definition~\ref{def:quadratic-parry-fold}.  Then
\[
m_*(\beta)=
\begin{cases}
3,&\beta\text{ is the root of }x^2-ax-a,\quad a\ge1,\\
3,&\beta\text{ is the root of }x^2-ax+1,\quad a\ge3,\\
2,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]
More strongly, for every $m\ge m_*(\beta)$ and every $n\ge m$, the
finite-block sliding fold is injective and
\begin{equation}
|\mathcal L_n(Y_{\beta,m})|
=(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+1)^{n+m-1}.
\label{eq:quadratic-pisot-block-count}
\end{equation}
At these apertures $\Phi_{\beta,m}$ is a topological conjugacy from the full
$(\lfloor\beta\rfloor+1)$-shift onto the SFT $Y_{\beta,m}$, with inverse of
memory zero and anticipation $m-1$.

Consequently the maximum of the threshold function on the full quadratic
Pisot family is three.  Its extremizers are exactly the negative-conjugate
diagonal $P^-_{a,a}$ and the positive-conjugate norm-one family
$P^+_{a,1}$.  The golden mean is the least extremizer, but it is not the
unique extremizer after passage from metallic units to all quadratic Pisot
bases.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Equality of two folded output blocks is, by
Proposition~\ref{prop:parry-rank-bijection}, equivalent to the sliding
congruences \eqref{eq:sliding-kernel} for their digit differences.  For
$m\ge3$, Lemma~\ref{lem:quadratic-sliding-congruences} makes every
length-$m$ finite-block map injective.  Consecutive decoded blocks cover a
longer raw block, proving injectivity for every $n\ge m$ and the count
\eqref{eq:quadratic-pisot-block-count}.  The standard gluing argument used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:metallic-threshold-classification} then gives a local inverse
and an SFT description by forbidden output words of length at most $m+1$.

It remains to classify $m=2$.  Put
\[
\kappa=Q_2-dQ_1=
\begin{cases}b,&P^-_{a,b},\\ a-b,&P^+_{a,b}.
\end{cases}
\]
Proposition~\ref{prop:parry-rank-bijection} gives the same explicit rule in
both chambers:
\[
\Fold_{\beta,2}(u,v)=
\begin{cases}
(u,v),&v<d\text{ or }u<\kappa,\\
(u-\kappa,0),&v=d\text{ and }u\ge\kappa.
\end{cases}
\]
If $d=\kappa$, the two constant raw points
$0^{\mathbb Z}$ and $d^{\mathbb Z}$ both map to the constant zero output,
so conjugacy fails.  This occurs exactly for $P^-_{a,a}$ and $P^+_{a,1}$.

Now assume $d>\kappa$, which is equivalent to $a>b$ in the negative chamber
and to $b>1$ in the positive chamber.  We prove finite-block injectivity,
including the boundary digits, rather than inferring it from a two-sided
inverse.  Let a raw triple $(a_0,a_1,a_2)$ produce
\[
y_i=(p_i,q_i)=\Fold_{\beta,2}(a_i,a_{i+1}),\qquad i=0,1.
\]
First recover the central digit from the two output symbols:
\begin{equation}
a_1=
\begin{cases}
q_0,&q_0>0,\\
0,&q_0=0\text{ and }p_1=0,\\
d,&q_0=0\text{ and }p_1\in\{d-\kappa,d\}.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:m2-central-decoder}
\end{equation}
Indeed, $q_0=0$ implies $a_1\in\{0,d\}$.  If $a_1=0$, the next pair has
first output coordinate $p_1=0$.  If $a_1=d$, then $p_1=d$ when
$a_2<d$ and $p_1=d-\kappa$ when $a_2=d$.  The alternatives are disjoint
because $d>\kappa>0$.

Having found $a_1$, recover the left boundary digit by
\begin{equation}
a_0=
\begin{cases}
p_0,&q_0>0,\\
p_0,&q_0=0\text{ and }a_1=0,\\
p_0+\kappa,&q_0=0\text{ and }a_1=d.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:m2-left-decoder}
\end{equation}
The last line is exactly the wrapped case in the explicit fold rule.  Finally,
recover the right boundary digit by
\begin{equation}
a_2=
\begin{cases}
q_1,&q_1>0,\\
0,&q_1=0\text{ and }p_1=a_1,\\
d,&q_1=0\text{ and }p_1=a_1-\kappa.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:m2-right-decoder}
\end{equation}
When $q_1=0$, the raw digit $a_2$ is $0$ or $d$; these give the last two
lines, which are disjoint because $\kappa>0$.  Equations
\eqref{eq:m2-central-decoder}--\eqref{eq:m2-right-decoder} prove directly that
\[
\Phi_{\beta,2,2}:\mathcal A_\beta^3
\longrightarrow (X_2^{(\beta)})^2
\]
is injective.

For any $n\ge2$, every consecutive pair $(y_t,y_{t+1})$ decodes the raw
triple $(a_t,a_{t+1},a_{t+2})$.  These triples cover the entire raw block
$a_0\ldots a_{n+1}$, so $\Phi_{\beta,2,n}$ is injective and
$|\mathcal L_n(Y_{\beta,2})|=(d+1)^{n+1}$.  On trajectories, using the first
coordinate of the triple decoded from $(y_t,y_{t+1})$ gives a genuine
memory-zero, anticipation-one inverse.

For the two extremal loci, Lemma~\ref{lem:quadratic-sliding-congruences} shows
that aperture three succeeds.  The chamber classification is exhaustive by
Lemma~\ref{lem:quadratic-pisot-chambers}, completing the proof.
\end{proof}



 succeeded in 199ms:
\subsection{Arithmetic identifiability and optimal local structure}

The threshold classification leaves three finer questions: whether a fixed
aperture identifies the parameter chamber, how many consecutive output
